# A new method for constructing a compact and non SI-compact space

**arXiv ID**: 2608.01048v1
**Authors**: Zhengmao He, Bin Zhao
**Published**: 2026-08-02
**Categories**: math.GN
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2608.01048v1

## Abstract

In this paper, we provide a new method for constructing a compact and non SI-compact space and thus give a new answer for the question posed by Zhao and Ho. Using this method, we find a countable compact space which fails to be SI-compact. As a corollary, some new non sober frames are obtained and thus we give a new answer for a question raised by A.Jung. Inspired by the definition of SI-compactness, we introduce a new notion of compactness of topological spaces, namely, v-compactness, which is stronger than SI-compactness.Some properties of v-compactness, SI-compactness and weak v-compactness are discussed. Specifically, we prove that (1) all closed subspaces of a v-compact(SI-compact,weakly v-compact) space are v-compact (SI-compact, weakly v-compact);(2) a characterization of v-compactness for the upper topology is given; (3)some characterizations of weakly v-compact subsets of real line and Sorgenfrey line are given.

A new method for constructing a compact and non SI-compact space

## A new method for constructing a compact and non SI-compact spaceZhengmao He, Bin ZhaoSchool of Mathematics and Statistics, Shaanxi Normal University,
Xi’an 710119, P.R. China

## Abstract

In this paper, we provide a new method for constructing a compact and nonS​ISI-compact space and thus give a new answer for the question posed by Zhao and Ho. Using this method, we find a countable compact space which fails to beS​ISI-compact. As a corollary, some new non sober frames are obtained and thus we give a new answer for a question raised by A. Jung. Inspired by the definition ofS​ISI-compactness, we introduce a new notion of compactness of topological spaces, namely,υ\upsilon-compactness, which is stronger thanS​ISI-compactness. Some properties ofυ\upsilon-compactness,S​ISI-compactness and weakυ\upsilon-compactness are discussed. Specifically, we prove that (1) all closed subspaces of aυ\upsilon-compact (S​ISI-compact, weaklyυ\upsilon-compact) space areυ\upsilon-compact (S​ISI-compact, weaklyυ\upsilon-compact); (2) a characterization ofυ\upsilon-compactness for the upper topology is given; (3) some characterizations of weaklyυ\upsilon-compact subsets of real line and Sorgenfrey line are given.

## keywords:S​ISI-compact;υ\upsilon-compact; weaklyυ\upsilon-compact; sober; Scott topologyMSC:06B35, 06B30, 54A05††This work is supported by the National Natural Science Foundation of China (No.12331016).††Corresponding author: Bin Zhao.††E-mail addresses: hezhengmao@snnu.edu.cn, zhaobin@snnu.edu.cn.

## 1Introduction

In Domain theory, there are serval order topologies which are frequently discussed by scholars, such as the upper topology, lower topology, Scott topology and Lawson topology of posets. In this paper, we will focus on the order topologies of open set lattice. As we know, compactness is one of the most important properties for topological spaces (see[7]). In[30], Zhao and Ho introduce a new type of compactness, namelyS​ISI-compactness which is defined by using the Scott topology of the open set lattice. Specifically, a topological spaceXXis calledS​ISI-compact if for every Scott irreducible subset𝒰\mathcal{U}of𝒪​(X)\mathcal{O}(X),⋃𝒰=X\bigcup\mathcal{U}=XimpliesX∈𝒰X\in\mathcal{U}. Note that A subsetFFof a topological spaceXXis irreducible if for each pair closed setsAAandBBofXX,F⊆A∪BF\subseteq A\cup BimpliesF⊆AF\subseteq AorF⊆BF\subseteq B. Inspired by the definition ofS​ISI-compactness, we propose a new
compactness, namely,𝒯\mathcal{T}-compactness which is defined by using the order compatible topology𝒯\mathcal{T}on the open set lattice. In this paper, we mainly considerS​ISI-compactness andυ\upsilon-compactness, where theυ\upsilon-compactness is defined by the upper topology of the open set lattice. The motivation for this study is based on two aspects. Firstly, from the perspective of general topology, the compactness induced by different order topologies is used to distinguish between supercompactness and compactness. This facilitates a more profound understanding of the strengths and weaknesses of several types of compactness. This serves to reinforce the connection between general topology and order theory, while also highlighting the significant role of order topology in general topology. In addition, by studyingS​ISI-compactness andυ\upsilon-compactness, we provide a new Scott non-Sober frame, which offers a new method to discuss sobriety for the Scott topology in Domain theory. This method provides a possibility for solving the problem raised by H. Miao, X. Xi, Q. Li and D. Zhao in[21].

In[30], Ho and Zhao find that everyS​ISI-compact space is always compact and they posed the following problem.

Problem 1: Is there a compact space which is notS​ISI-compact?

In[13], we first solve problem 1 by giving a compact and notS​ISI-compact space. However, that space is uncountable. The following problem arises naturally.

Problem 2: Is there a countable compact space which is notS​ISI-compact?

In this paper, we give a new example which is countable compact but notS​ISI-compact space by using the lower topology of posets. Furthermore, using this example, a new method for constructing a frame whose Scott space is non sober for the Scott topology is obtained and then we solve the following problem 3 again which is proposed by A. Jung in[2]. We call a topological spaceXXis sober if every irreducible closed set ofXXis the closure of a singleton point.

Problem 3: Is there a distributive complete lattice whose Scott space is non-sober?

The above 3 problems play an important role in strengthening the further connection among order theory, general topology and Domain theory. Concretely, these 3 problems has a great effect on solving some of open problems related to sobriety in Domain theory. The problem 3 is first solved by Xu, Xi and Zhao in[27]. Concretely, they prove that the Scott space of the frame𝒬​(L)\mathcal{Q}(L)is non sober, where𝒬​(L)\mathcal{Q}(L)is the poset of all nonempty compact saturated subsets of the Scott space of Isbell’s complete lattice with inverse inclusion order. In addition, the examples of non-sobriety for the Scott topology were given by many scholars(see[16,17,20,28,31]). In this paper, we give a new answer for the problem 3. Specifically, we proved that the lower topology of the dcpoP^\widehat{P}is non sober for the Scott topology, where the dcpoP^\widehat{P}is constructed by Miao, xi, Li and Zhao in[21]. Furthermore, we know that there is a countableT1T_{1}topological spaceXXsuch that the open set lattice𝒪​(X)\mathcal{O}(X)is non sober for the Scott topology. In addition, we proved that the closed subspaces of aS​ISI-compact space areS​ISI-compact in this paper. Unfortunately, the open subspaces of aS​ISI-compact space are notS​ISI-compact in general.

We further discuss the properties ofυ\upsilon-compactness. It is proved thatυ\upsilon-compactness is stronger than compactness and they are equivalent in locally hypercompact spaces. In addition, we show that every closed subspace of aυ\upsilon-compact space isυ\upsilon-compact and every topological space is an open subspace of aυ\upsilon-compact space. Besides, the characterization of theυ\upsilon-compactness of the upper topology is obtained. Furthermore, we find that aT1T_{1}spaceXXisυ\upsilon-compact if and only ifXXis finite. So we modify the definition ofυ\upsilon-compactness. The modified compactness (called weakυ\upsilon-compactness) can hold in infinite Hausdorff spaces. Simultaneously, we characterize the weaklyυ\upsilon-compact subsets of the real line and Sorgenfrey line.

## 3The compactness defined by the compatible topology

In this section, we introduce some new kinds of compactness by using the order compatible topology on open set lattice and discuss the properties of these kinds of compactness.

## Definition 3.1.

LetXXbe a topological space and𝒯\mathcal{T}an order compatible topology on𝒪​(X)\mathcal{O}(X).XXis called𝒯\mathcal{T}-compact if for every open cover𝒰\mathcal{U}ofXXwhich is irreducible in the topological space(𝒪​(X),𝒯)(\mathcal{O}(X),\mathcal{T}),⋃𝒰=X\bigcup\mathcal{U}=XimpliesX∈𝒰X\in\mathcal{U}.

## Remark 3.2.

(1) Note that the irreducible subsets in Alexandroff topology are exactly all directed subsets. ThusXXisα​(𝒪​(X))\alpha(\mathcal{O}(X))-compact iffXXis compact iff every directed open cover𝒰\mathcal{U}containsXX. Furthermore,XXis always compact ifXXis𝒯\mathcal{T}-compact, where𝒯\mathcal{T}is an order compatible topology on𝒪​(X)\mathcal{O}(X).

(2) Given a topological spaceXXand𝒯1,𝒯2\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{2}two order compatible topologies on𝒪​(X)\mathcal{O}(X)with𝒯1⊆𝒯2\mathcal{T}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{2}. ThenXXis𝒯2\mathcal{T}_{2}compact ifXXis𝒯1\mathcal{T}_{1}compact.

(3) LetXXbe a topological space and𝒯\mathcal{T}a order compatible topology on𝒪​(X)\mathcal{O}(X). If(𝒪​(X),𝒯)(\mathcal{O}(X),\mathcal{T})is hyper-sober, thenXXis𝒯\mathcal{T}-compact. As a matter of fact, for every𝒯\mathcal{T}-irreducible open cover𝒰\mathcal{U}ofXX, there is aU∈𝒰U\in\mathcal{U}such that𝒰⊆c​l𝒯​({U})\mathcal{U}\subseteq cl_{\mathcal{T}}(\{U\})since(𝒪​(X),𝒯)(\mathcal{O}(X),\mathcal{T})is hyper-sober. Then we can seeU⊆⋃𝒰=X⊆⋃c​l𝒯​({U})=U.U\subseteq\bigcup\mathcal{U}=X\subseteq\bigcup cl_{\mathcal{T}}(\{U\})=U.

Whence,U=X∈𝒰U=X\in\mathcal{U}. Hence,XXis𝒯\mathcal{T}-compact.

For every posetPP, a nonempty subsetDDis countably directed if for any countbale subsetD0⊆DD_{0}\subseteq D, there is ad∈Dd\in Dsuch thatD0⊆↓​dD_{0}\subseteq\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003ptd. A subsetU⊆PU\subseteq Pisσ𝒞\sigma_{\mathcal{C}}-Scott open if (i)UU=↑​U=\mathord{\uparrow}\hskip 0.50003ptUand (ii) for any countably directed setDD,⋁D∈U\bigvee D\in UimpliesD∩U≠∅D\cap U\neq\emptysetwhenever⋁D\bigvee Dexists. The the set of allσ𝒞\sigma_{\mathcal{C}}-Scott open subsets ofPPis denoted byσ𝒞​(P)\sigma_{\mathcal{C}}(P). Thenσ𝒞​(P)\sigma_{\mathcal{C}}(P)is still an order compatible topology onPP(see[10,19,29]). The space(P,σ𝒞​(P))(P,\sigma_{\mathcal{C}}(P))will be simply written asΣ𝒞​P\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}P.

In the following, a𝒯\mathcal{T}-compact spaceXXis calledυ\upsilon-compact,σ𝒞\sigma_{\mathcal{C}}-compact andS​ISI-compact if𝒯\mathcal{T}is equals toυ​(𝒪​(X))\upsilon(\mathcal{O}(X)),σ𝒞​(𝒪​(X))\sigma_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{O}(X))andσ​(𝒪​(X))\sigma(\mathcal{O}(X)), respectively.

## Proposition 3.3.

Letf:X⟶Yf:X\longrightarrow Ybe a surjective continuous map andXXaσ𝒞\sigma_{\mathcal{C}}-compact space. ThenYYisσ𝒞\sigma_{\mathcal{C}}-compact.

## Proof.

Let𝒰⊆𝒪​(Y)\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(Y)be an irreducible open over ofYYwith respect toσ𝒞​(𝒪​(Y))\sigma_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{O}(Y)). Consider the mapg:Σ𝒞​𝒪​(Y)⟶Σ𝒞​𝒪​(X)g:\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{O}(Y)\longrightarrow\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{O}(X)which sendsUUtof−1​(U)f^{-1}(U). Thenggis continuous forggpreserves the suprema of all countably directed subsets. Henceg​(𝒰)g(\mathcal{U})is irreducible and⋃g​(𝒰)=X\bigcup g(\mathcal{U})=Xsinceffis surjective. AsXXisσ𝒞\sigma_{\mathcal{C}}-compact,X∈g​(𝒰)X\in g(\mathcal{U}). It follows thatY∈𝒰Y\in\mathcal{U}. SoYYisσ𝒞\sigma_{\mathcal{C}}-compact.
∎

## Theorem 3.4.

LetXXbe a topological space. IfΣ𝒞​𝒪​(X)\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{O}(X)is sober, thenXXis compact iffXXisσ𝒞\sigma_{\mathcal{C}}-compact.

## Proof.

It suffices to show the necessity. Let𝒰\mathcal{U}be an irreducible subset with respect toΣ𝒞​𝒪​(X)\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{O}(X)and⋃𝒰=X\bigcup\mathcal{U}=X. Assume thatX∉𝒰X\not\in\mathcal{U}. Clearly,𝒪​(X)∖{X}\mathcal{O}(X)\setminus\{X\}is a lower set in𝒪​(X)\mathcal{O}(X). Letℬ\mathcal{B}be a countably directed subset of𝒪​(X)∖{X}\mathcal{O}(X)\setminus\{X\}. SinceXXis compact, by Remark 3.2(1), we have that⋃ℬ∈𝒪​(X)∖{X}\bigcup\mathcal{B}\in\mathcal{O}(X)\setminus\{X\}. Then𝒪​(X)∖{X}\mathcal{O}(X)\setminus\{X\}is closed inΣ𝒞​𝒪​(X)\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{O}(X). Since𝒰\mathcal{U}is irreducible inΣ𝒞​𝒪​(X)\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{O}(X)and𝒰⊆𝒪​(X)∖{X}\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(X)\setminus\{X\},c​lΣ​(𝒪​(X))​(𝒰)cl_{\Sigma(\mathcal{O}(X))}(\mathcal{U})is a irreducible closed set inΣ𝒞​𝒪​(X)\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{O}(X)andc​lΣ𝒞​(𝒪​(X))​(𝒰)⊆𝒪​(X)∖{X}cl_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{O}(X))}(\mathcal{U})\subseteq\mathcal{O}(X)\setminus\{X\}. It follows from the sobriety ofΣ𝒞​𝒪​(X)\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{O}(X)that there exists a unique open setAAofXXsuch thatc​lΣ𝒞​𝒪​(X)​(𝒰)=c​lΣ𝒞​𝒪​(X)​{A}=↓𝒪​(X)​A⊆𝒪​(X)∖{X}.cl_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{O}(X)}(\mathcal{U})=cl_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{O}(X)}\{A\}=\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003pt_{\mathcal{O}(X)}A\subseteq\mathcal{O}(X)\setminus\{X\}.

SinceX=⋃𝒰⊆⋃c​lΣ​(𝒪​(X))​(𝒰)=A,X=\bigcup\mathcal{U}\subseteq\bigcup cl_{\Sigma(\mathcal{O}(X))}(\mathcal{U})=A,

we haveX=AX=A. It is a contradiction. ThusXXisσ𝒞\sigma_{\mathcal{C}}-compact.∎

For a complete latticeLL, we let𝒪​(L^)={L^∖K∣K∈𝒬​(L)}\mathcal{O}(\hat{L})=\{\hat{L}\setminus K\mid K\in\mathcal{Q}(L)\}, whereL^=L∖{1L}\hat{L}=L\setminus\{1_{L}\}and1L1_{L}is the top element ofLL. Then(L^,𝒪​(L^))(\hat{L},\mathcal{O}(\hat{L}))is a topological space and𝒬​(L)\mathcal{Q}(L)is order isomorphic to the open set lattice(𝒪​(L^),⊆)(\mathcal{O}(\hat{L}),\subseteq)(see[13]). Actually,(L^,𝒪​(L^))(\hat{L},\mathcal{O}(\hat{L}))is constructed by using the work of W. Thron (see[23]).

## Lemma 3.5.

(see[13]) LetXXbe a topological space. IfΣ​𝒪​(X)\Sigma\mathcal{O}(X)is sober, thenXXis compact iffXXisS​ISI-compact.

## Lemma 3.6.

(see[13]) LetLLbe the Isbell complete lattice. Then we have the following statements

(1){\rm(1)}(L^,𝒪​(L^))(\hat{L},\mathcal{O}(\hat{L}))is compact;

(2){\rm(2)}𝒰=𝒪​(L^)∖{L^}\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{O}(\hat{L})\setminus\{\hat{L}\}is irreducible with respect to the Scott topology of𝒪​(L^)\mathcal{O}(\hat{L})and⋃𝒰=L^\bigcup\mathcal{U}=\hat{L}.

Note that for aS​ISI-compact spaceXX,Σ​𝒪​(X)\Sigma\mathcal{O}(X)is non sober in general. See the following example.

## Example 3.7.

LetXXbe the topological space given in Lemma 3.6. ThenXXis an uncountable compact space and𝒰=𝒪​(X)∖{X}\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{O}(X)\setminus\{X\}is a Scott irreducible open cover ofXX. Take a∞∉X\infty\not\in Xand letY=X∪{∞}Y=X\cup\{\infty\}. The topologyτ\tauonYYis given byτ=𝒪​(X)∪{Y}.\tau=\mathcal{O}(X)\cup\{Y\}.

Obviously, the inclusion mapi:𝒪​(X)⟶𝒪​(Y)i:\mathcal{O}(X)\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}(Y)is Scott continuous and(Y,τ)(Y,\tau)isS​ISI-compact(actually, supercompact). Theni​(𝒰)=𝒰i(\mathcal{U})=\mathcal{U}is still irreducible inΣ​𝒪​(Y)\Sigma\mathcal{O}(Y). SinceXXand(Y,τ)(Y,\tau)are compact,𝒰=↓​𝒰\mathcal{U}=\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathcal{U}is Scott irreducible closed inΣ​𝒪​(Y)\Sigma\mathcal{O}(Y). However,↓​𝒰≠c​lσ​(𝒪​(Y))​{U}\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathcal{U}\neq cl_{\sigma(\mathcal{O}(Y))}\{U\}for allU∈𝒪​(Y)U\in\mathcal{O}(Y). Thus,Σ​𝒪​(Y)\Sigma\mathcal{O}(Y)is non-sober.

## Example 3.8.

LetLLbe the Isbell complete lattice (see[15]). It has proved in[15]thatΣ​L\Sigma Lis non sober. By Lemma 3.6,(L^,𝒪​(L^))(\hat{L},\mathcal{O}(\hat{L}))is compact but notS​ISI-compact. Actually, by the proof of Example 4.6 in[13], we see that𝒰=𝒪​(L^)∖{L^}\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{O}(\hat{L})\setminus\{\hat{L}\}is also irreducible with respect to(𝒪​(L^),σ𝒞​𝒪​(L^))(\mathcal{O}(\hat{L}),\sigma_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{O}(\hat{L}))and⋃𝒰=L^\bigcup\mathcal{U}=\hat{L}. Therefore,(L^,𝒪​(L^))(\hat{L},\mathcal{O}(\hat{L}))is compact but notσ𝒞\sigma_{\mathcal{C}}-compact.

## Corollary 3.9.

LetLLbe the Isbell complete lattice. ThenΣ𝒞​𝒪​(L^)\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{O}(\hat{L})is non-sober.

## Proof.

It is straightforward by using Theorem 3.4 and Example 3.8.∎

A topological spaceXXis called co-consonant if for everyℱ∈σ​(𝒪​(X))\mathcal{F}\in\sigma(\mathcal{O}(X))andU∈ℱU\in\mathcal{F}, there is a finite subsetℰ⊆Γ​(X)\mathcal{E}\subseteq\Gamma(X)such thatU∈⋂{◆​A∣A∈ℰ}⊆ℱ,U\in\bigcap\{\lozenge A\mid A\in\mathcal{E}\}\subseteq\mathcal{F},

where◆​A={V∈𝒪​(X)∣A∩V≠∅}\lozenge A=\{V\in\mathcal{O}(X)\mid A\cap V\neq\emptyset\}. Equivalently,XXis co-consonant if and only if the upper topology and the Scott topology on𝒪​(X)\mathcal{O}(X)coincide (see[3]). It is evident that aS​ISI-compact spaceXXisυ\upsilon-compact ifXXis co-consonant. Given a finite topological spaceYY, it is clear that the upper topology and Alexandroff topology on𝒪​(Y)\mathcal{O}(Y)coincide. ThusYYis alwaysυ\upsilon-compact. Generally, we have the following Proposition 3.10.

## Proposition 3.10.

LetXXbe a locally hypercompact space. ThenXXisυ\upsilon-compact iffXXis compact.

## Proof.

Necessity: Obvious.

Sufficiency: Clearly,Σ​𝒪​(X)\Sigma\mathcal{O}(X)is sober sinceXXis locally compact. According to Lemma 3.5,XXisS​ISI-compact. Then the proof is complete by checking thatXXis co-consonant. Let𝒰⊆𝒪​(X)\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(X)be a Scott open set andU∈𝒰U\in\mathcal{U}. Without loss of generality, we supposeUUis nonempty.
AsXXis a locally hypercompact compact space, we haveU=⋃𝒜U=\bigcup\mathcal{A}, where𝒜={i​n​t​(↑​F)∣F∈𝐅𝐢𝐧​(U)}.\mathcal{A}=\{int(\mathord{\uparrow}\hskip 0.50003ptF)\mid F\in\mathbf{Fin}(U)\}.

Since𝒰\mathcal{U}is Scott open and𝒜\mathcal{A}is directed, there is aF0∈𝐅𝐢𝐧​(U)F_{0}\in\mathbf{Fin}(U)such thati​n​t​(↑​F0)∈𝒰int(\mathord{\uparrow}\hskip 0.50003ptF_{0})\in\mathcal{U}. Consider𝒱={V∈𝒪​(X)∣F0⊆V}=𝒪​(X)∖↓𝒪​(X)​{X∖↓​f∣f∈F0}.\mathcal{V}=\{V\in\mathcal{O}(X)\mid F_{0}\subseteq V\}=\mathcal{O}(X)\setminus\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003pt_{\mathcal{O}(X)}\{X\setminus\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003ptf\mid f\in F_{0}\}.

Clearly,U∈𝒱U\in\mathcal{V}and𝒱\mathcal{V}is open with respect to the upper topology on𝒪​(X)\mathcal{O}(X). IfW∈𝒱W\in\mathcal{V}, theni​n​t​(↑​F0)⊆↑​F0⊆Wint(\mathord{\uparrow}\hskip 0.50003ptF_{0})\subseteq\mathord{\uparrow}\hskip 0.50003ptF_{0}\subseteq W. It follows fromi​n​t​(↑​F0)∈𝒰∈σ​(𝒪​(X))int(\mathord{\uparrow}\hskip 0.50003ptF_{0})\in\mathcal{U}\in\sigma(\mathcal{O}(X))thatW∈𝒰W\in\mathcal{U}. Consequently,U∈𝒱⊆𝒰U\in\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{U}and then𝒰∈υ​(𝒪​(X))\mathcal{U}\in\upsilon(\mathcal{O}(X)). Thus,Σ​P\Sigma Pis co-consonant.∎

LetXXbe a topological space. We consider three topologies on𝒪​(X)\mathcal{O}(X). They areσ​(𝒪​(X))\sigma(\mathcal{O}(X)),υ​(𝒪​(X))\upsilon(\mathcal{O}(X))and the topologyτX\tau_{X}having a subbase{□​Q∣Q∈𝒬​(X)},\{\square Q\mid Q\in\mathcal{Q}(X)\},

where□​Q={V∈𝒪​(X)∣Q⊆V}\square Q=\{V\in\mathcal{O}(X)\mid Q\subseteq V\}. It is clear thatυ​(𝒪​(X))⊆τX⊆σ​(𝒪​(X))\upsilon(\mathcal{O}(X))\subseteq\tau_{X}\subseteq\sigma(\mathcal{O}(X)). We sayXXis consonant (co-consonant) ifτX=σ​(𝒪​(X))\tau_{X}=\sigma(\mathcal{O}(X))(υ​(𝒪​(X))=σ​(𝒪​(X))\upsilon(\mathcal{O}(X))=\sigma(\mathcal{O}(X))). It is easy to see that every co-consonant space is consonant.

## Lemma 3.11.

(see[4])XXis locally compact if and only ifXXis core-compact and consonant.

According to the proof of Proposition 3.10, every locally hypercompact space is co-consonant. So it is natural to ask whether every core-compact and co-consonant space is locally hypercompact. In the following, we give a positive answer in Proposition 3.13.

## Lemma 3.12.

(see[3]) LetXXbe a co-consonantT0T_{0}space. Then every compact subset ofXXis strongly compact.

## Proposition 3.13.

A topological spaceXXis locally hypercompact if and only ifXXis core-compact and co-consonant.

## Proof.

Necessity: Obviously.

Sufficiency: LetU∈𝒪​(X)U\in\mathcal{O}(X)andx∈Ux\in U. Since every co-consonant space is consonant, by Lemma 3.11,XXis locally compact. Then there is aV∈𝒪​(X)V\in\mathcal{O}(X)andK∈𝒬​(X)K\in\mathcal{Q}(X)such thatx∈V⊆K⊆Ux\in V\subseteq K\subseteq U. By Lemma 3.12,XXis strongly compact. This means thatK⊆↑​FK\subseteq\mathord{\uparrow}\hskip 0.50003ptFfor some finite subsetF⊆UF\subseteq U. Consequently,x∈V⊆i​n​t​(↑​F)⊆↑​F⊆U.x\in V\subseteq int(\mathord{\uparrow}\hskip 0.50003ptF)\subseteq\mathord{\uparrow}\hskip 0.50003ptF\subseteq U.

ThereforeXXis locally hypercompact.∎

Note that the Alexandroff topology of a poset is always locally hypercompact. So we have the following Corollary 3.14.

## Corollary 3.14.

LetPPbe a poset. The following statements are equivalent.

(1)Σ​P\Sigma Pis compact;

(2)(P,α​(P))(P,\alpha(P))is compact;

(3)(P,α​(P))(P,\alpha(P))isυ\upsilon-compact;

(4)P=↑​FP=\mathord{\uparrow}\hskip 0.50003ptFfor some finite subsetF⊆PF\subseteq P.

## Proof.

(1)⟹\Longrightarrow(4): Using Lemma 2.2, we haveP=↑​min​(P)P=\mathord{\uparrow}\hskip 0.50003pt{\rm min(P)}andmin​(P){\rm min(P)}is a compact subset ofΣ​P\Sigma P. Assume thatmin​(P){\rm min(P)}is infinite. Consider𝒮={P∖E∣E⊆min​(P),min​(P)∖E∈𝐅𝐢𝐧​(min​(P))}.\mathcal{S}=\{P\setminus E\mid E\subseteq{\rm min(P)},{\rm min(P)}\setminus E\in\mathbf{Fin}({\rm min(P)})\}.

Then𝒮⊆σ​(P)\mathcal{S}\subseteq\sigma(P)is directed and⋃𝒮=P\bigcup\mathcal{S}=P. However,P∉𝒮P\not\in\mathcal{S}, a contradiction. Thus,min​(P){\rm min(P)}is finite.

(2)⟺\Longleftrightarrow(3): By Proposition 3.10.

(2)⟹\Longrightarrow(1),(4)⟹\Longrightarrow(1) and (4)⟹\Longrightarrow(2): Clearly.
∎

For every continuous posetPP,Σ​P\Sigma Pis always locally hypercompact and hence by proposition 3.10,Σ​P\Sigma Pis co-consonant. It is proved in[14]that for a continuous posetPP,PH​(Σ​P)P_{H}(\Sigma P)is still co-consonant. In[14], He and Zhao asked whether the lower powerspace of a core-compact and co-consonant space is co-consonant. We will give a solution for this problem in Theorem 3.15.

## Example 3.16.

LetP={an∣n∈ℕ}∪{bn∣n∈ℕ}∪{∞,b}P=\{a_{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}\cup\{b_{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}\cup\{\infty,b\}. The order≤\leqonPPis given by

In the following, we present an example which is compact but notυ\upsilon-compact. The following Lemma is useful and it has been proved in[18].

## Lemma 3.17.

(see[18]) LetPPbe a poset. Then the following statements are equivalent:

(1)(P,ω​(P))(P,\omega(P))is compact;

(2)Every nonempty subsetA⊆PA\subseteq Phas an upper bound inPPif every finite
subset ofAAhas an upper bound inPP.

## Theorem 3.18.

LetPPbe a poset. Then(P,υ​(P))(P,\upsilon(P))isυ\upsilon-compact iffP=↑​FP=\mathord{\uparrow}\hskip 0.50003ptFfor some finite subsetFFofPP.

## Proof.

Sufficiency: LetF1=min​(F)F_{1}={\rm min(F)}. ThenP=↑​F1P=\mathord{\uparrow}\hskip 0.50003ptF_{1}andmin​(P)=F1{\rm min(P)}=F_{1}is finite. Let𝒰⊆υ​(P)\mathcal{U}\subseteq\upsilon(P)be an irreducible open cover ofPPwith respect to the upper topology ofυ​(P)\upsilon(P). Assume thatP∉𝒰P\not\in\mathcal{U}. Then for everyU∈𝒰U\in\mathcal{U},U⊆P∖↓​x=P∖{x}U\subseteq P\setminus\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003ptx=P\setminus\{x\}for somex∈F1x\in F_{1}. Equivalently,𝒰⊆⋃x∈F1↓υ​(P)​P∖↓​x.\mathcal{U}\subseteq\bigcup\limits_{x\in F_{1}}\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003pt_{\upsilon(P)}P\setminus\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003ptx.

By the irreduciblity of𝒰\mathcal{U},𝒰⊆↓υ​(P)​P∖↓​x0\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003pt_{\upsilon(P)}P\setminus\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003ptx_{0}for somex0∈F1x_{0}\in F_{1}. This implies⋃𝒰⊆P∖↓​x0\bigcup\mathcal{U}\subseteq P\setminus\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003ptx_{0}, a contradiction. ThusP∈𝒰P\in\mathcal{U}and then(P,υ​(P))(P,\upsilon(P))isυ\upsilon-compact.

Necessity: Since(P,υ​(P))(P,\upsilon(P))isυ\upsilon-compact,(P,υ​(P))(P,\upsilon(P))is compact. Then by Lemma 2.2,P=↑​min​(P)P=\mathord{\uparrow}\hskip 0.50003pt{\rm min(P)}andmin​(P){\rm min(P)}is compact in(P,υ​(P))(P,\upsilon(P)). Assume thatmin​(P){\rm min(P)}is infinite. Consider𝒮={P∖↓​a∣a∈min​(P)}.\mathcal{S}=\{P\setminus\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003pta\mid a\in{\rm min(P)}\}.

Clearly,𝒮⊆υ​(P)\mathcal{S}\subseteq\upsilon(P)and⋃𝒮=P\bigcup\mathcal{S}=P. IfP∉c​lυ​(υ​(P))​(𝒮)P\not\in cl_{\upsilon(\upsilon(P))}(\mathcal{S}), then there exist{ℱi∣i∈I}⊆𝐅𝐢𝐧​(υ​(P))\{\mathcal{F}_{i}\mid i\in I\}\subseteq\mathbf{Fin}(\upsilon(P))such thatc​lυ​(υ​(P))​(𝒮)=⋂i∈I↓υ​(P)​ℱi.cl_{\upsilon(\upsilon(P))}(\mathcal{S})=\bigcap\limits_{i\in I}\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003pt_{\upsilon(P)}\mathcal{F}_{i}.

It follows that𝒮⊆↓υ​(P)​ℱi\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003pt_{\upsilon(P)}\mathcal{F}_{i}, for alli∈Ii\in I.
Note that every element of𝒮\mathcal{S}is maximal inυ​(P)∖{P}\upsilon(P)\setminus\{P\}. Sincemin​(P){\rm min(P)}is infinite andℱi\mathcal{F}_{i}is finite,P∈ℱiP\in\mathcal{F}_{i}for alli∈Ii\in I. Whence,c​lυ​(υ​(P))​(𝒮)=⋂i∈I↓υ​(P)​ℱi=↓υ​(P)​P=υ​(P).cl_{\upsilon(\upsilon(P))}(\mathcal{S})=\bigcap\limits_{i\in I}\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003pt_{\upsilon(P)}\mathcal{F}_{i}=\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003pt_{\upsilon(P)}P=\upsilon(P).

This means that𝒮\mathcal{S}is irreducible in the upper topology ofυ​(P)\upsilon(P). As(P,υ​(P))(P,\upsilon(P))isυ\upsilon-compact,P∈𝒮P\in\mathcal{S}. It is a contradiction. Thus,min​(P){\rm min(P)}is finite.
∎

## Example 3.19.

LetP=ℕ∪{∞}P=\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}, whereℕ\mathbb{N}is the natural numbers set. The order≤\leqonPPis given by∀x∈P,x≤∞.\forall\ x\in P,x\leq\infty.

LetY=(P,υ​(P))Y=(P,\upsilon(P)). ThenYYis compact and locally compact sober. ThusΣ​𝒪​(Y)\Sigma\mathcal{O}(Y)is sober and by Lemma 3.5,YYisS​ISI-compact. Clearly, we haveυ​(P)={∅,P}∪{P∖F∣F​is a nonempty finite subset of​ℕ}.\upsilon(P)=\{\emptyset,P\}\cup\{P\setminus F\mid F\ \mbox{is a nonempty finite subset of}\ \mathbb{N}\}.

So every directed subset of𝒪​(Y)\mathcal{O}(Y)contains a largest element. This means that the Scott topology agrees with the Alexandroff topology on𝒪​(Y)\mathcal{O}(Y). However, the upper topology on𝒪​(Y)\mathcal{O}(Y)is strictly less than the Alexandroff topology. Hence,YYis not co-consonant. Note thatmin​(P)=ℕ{\rm min(P)}=\mathbb{N}is infinite. Hence by Theorem 3.18,YYis notυ\upsilon-compact.

Unfortunately,υ\upsilon-compactness does not hold for all infiniteT1T_{1}spaces. See the following Proposition 3.20.

## Proposition 3.20.

LetXXbe aT1T_{1}space. IfXXisυ\upsilon-compact, thenXXis finite.

## Proof.

Assume thatXXis infinite. Let𝒰={X∖{x}∣x∈X}\mathcal{U}=\{X\setminus\{x\}\mid x\in X\}. Then𝒰\mathcal{U}coversXX. Ifc​lυ​(𝒪​(X))​(𝒰)=⋂i∈I↓​ℱicl_{\upsilon(\mathcal{O}(X))}(\mathcal{U})=\bigcap\limits_{i\in I}\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathcal{F}_{i}for some{ℱi∣i∈I}⊆𝐅𝐢𝐧​(𝒪​(X))\{\mathcal{F}_{i}\mid i\in I\}\subseteq\mathbf{Fin}(\mathcal{O}(X)), then𝒰⊆↓​ℱi\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathcal{F}_{i}. SinceXXis infinite and every member of𝒰\mathcal{U}is maximal in𝒪​(X)∖{X}\mathcal{O}(X)\setminus\{X\},X∈⋂i∈IℱiX\in\bigcap\limits_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}_{i}. Hence, we havec​lυ​(𝒪​(X))​(𝒰)=⋂i∈I↓​ℱi=𝒪​(X).cl_{\upsilon(\mathcal{O}(X))}(\mathcal{U})=\bigcap\limits_{i\in I}\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathcal{F}_{i}=\mathcal{O}(X).

This means that𝒰\mathcal{U}is irreducible in the upper topology of𝒪​(X)\mathcal{O}(X). This contradicts theυ\upsilon-compactness ofXX. The finiteness ofXXis obtained.∎

## 4A new method for getting Scott non sober frames

In the following, we consider theS​ISI-compactness of the lower topology, Scott topology, and Lawson topology of posets. Using the above conclusions, we obtain some new frames which are non sober for the Scott topology.

## Lemma 4.1.

(see[8]) LetLLbe a complete lattice. IfΣ​(L×L)=Σ​L×Σ​L\Sigma(L\times L)=\Sigma L\times\Sigma L, thenΣ​L\Sigma Lis sober.

By Lemma 3.5 and Lemma 4.1, we have have the following Corollary 4.2 immediately.

## Corollary 4.2.

LetPPbe a poset. If(P,υ​(P))(P,\upsilon(P))is compact andΣ​(υ​(P)×υ​(P))=Σ​υ​(P)×Σ​υ​(P),\Sigma(\upsilon(P)\times\upsilon(P))=\Sigma\upsilon(P)\times\Sigma\upsilon(P),

then(P,υ​(P))(P,\upsilon(P))isS​ISI-compact.

The following Theorem 4.3 is is useful for proving the compactness and nonS​ISI-compactness of some spaces. Specifically, an infinite countable example will be given using the lower topology.

## Theorem 4.3.

LetLLbe a dcpo. IfMax​(L){\rm Max(L)}is irreducible inΣ​L\Sigma L, then(L,ω​(L))(L,\omega(L))isS​ISI-compact iffMax​(L){\rm Max(L)}is finite.

## Proof.

Sufficiency: Straightforward using Theorem 3.18.

Necessity: Assume thatMax​(L){\rm Max(L)}is infinite. Consider𝒮={L∖↑​x∣x∈Max​(L)}\mathcal{S}=\{L\setminus\mathord{\uparrow}\hskip 0.50003ptx\mid x\in{\rm Max(L)}\}. Clearly,𝒮⊆ω​(L)\mathcal{S}\subseteq\omega(L)and⋃𝒮=L\bigcup\mathcal{S}=L. Define a mapf:L⟶ω​(L)f:L\longrightarrow\omega(L)as follows:∀x∈L,f​(x)=L∖↑​x.\forall\ x\in L,f(x)=L\setminus\mathord{\uparrow}\hskip 0.50003ptx.

LetD⊆LD\subseteq Lbe a directed subset. Then we have⋁ω​(L)f​(D)=⋃d∈DL∖↑​d=L∖⋂d∈D↑​d=L∖↑​⋁D=f​(⋁D).\bigvee_{\omega(L)}f(D)=\bigcup\limits_{d\in D}L\setminus\mathord{\uparrow}\hskip 0.50003ptd=L\setminus\bigcap\limits_{d\in D}\mathord{\uparrow}\hskip 0.50003ptd=L\setminus\mathord{\uparrow}\hskip 0.50003pt\bigvee D=f(\bigvee D).

Thusffis Scott continuous.
Sof​(Max​(L))=𝒮f({\rm Max(L)})=\mathcal{S}is irreducible inΣ​ω​(L)\Sigma\omega(L). As(L,ω​(L))(L,\omega(L))isS​ISI-compact,L∈𝒮L\in\mathcal{S}, a contradiction. ThusMax​(L){\rm Max(L)}is finite.∎

## Corollary 4.4.

LetLLbe a dcpo withω≤|Max​(L)|\omega\leq|{\rm Max(L)}|. IfMax​(L){\rm Max(L)}is irreducible inΣ​L\Sigma Land(L,ω​(L))(L,\omega(L))is compact, thenΣ​ω​(L)\Sigma\omega(L)is non-sober.

## Proof.

By Lemma 3.5 and Theorem 4.3,Σ​ω​(L)\Sigma\omega(L)is non-sober.∎

In the following, we present two new examples which are compact but notS​ISI-compact by using the lower topology.

The other example which is similar to the topological space(P∗,ω​(P∗))(P^{\ast},\omega(P^{\ast}))is the lower topology on the dcpoP^\widehat{P}constructed by Miao, Xi, Li and Zhao in[21]. Furthermore, the Dedekind-MacNeille completion ofP^\widehat{P}is a countable complete lattice which is non sober for the Scott topology (see[21]). First we need to recall to definition of the dcpoP^\hat{P}.

## Example 4.6.

(see[21])
Letℕ<ω\mathbb{N}^{<\omega}be the set of all nonempty finite sequences overℕ\mathbb{N}. The
order≤\leqonℕ<ω\mathbb{N}^{<\omega}is the usual prefix order.

Fors=x1​x2​⋯​xn∈ℕ<ωs=x_{1}x_{2}\cdot\cdot\cdot x_{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{<\omega}, the length ofssis denoted byl​e​n​(s)len(s)and hencel​e​n​(s)=nlen(s)=n.

Let{p1,p2,⋯,pn,⋯}\{p_{1},p_{2},\cdot\cdot\cdot,p_{n},\cdot\cdot\cdot\}be the set of all prime numbers ofℕ\mathbb{N}.

(i)ℕ\mathbb{N}can be expressed as the union of countably disjoint infinite subsets ofℕ\mathbb{N}.

LetE1={pn∣1≤n}∪{1}E_{1}=\{p_{n}\mid 1\leq n\}\cup\{1\}and for every2≤k2\leq kEk={pr1×pr2×⋯×prk∣1≤r1,r2,⋯,rk∈ℕ}.E_{k}=\{p_{r_{1}}\times p_{r_{2}}\times\cdot\cdot\cdot\times p_{r_{k}}\mid 1\leq r_{1},r_{2},\cdot\cdot\cdot,r_{k}\in\mathbb{N}\}.

For example,E2={2×2,2×3,2×5,⋯},E_{2}=\{2\times 2,2\times 3,2\times 5,\cdot\cdot\cdot\},E3={2×2×2,2×2×3,2×2×5,⋯}.E_{3}=\{2\times 2\times 2,2\times 2\times 3,2\times 2\times 5,\cdot\cdot\cdot\}.

Then we can see⋃n∈ℕEn=ℕ\bigcup\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}E_{n}=\mathbb{N}andEm∩En=∅E_{m}\cap E_{n}=\emptysetfor allm≠nm\neq n.

(ii) There is a bijectionh:ℕ×ℕ⟶ℕh:\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}such that for allm,n∈ℕm,n\in\mathbb{N},m<h​(m,n)m<h(m,n)andn<h​(m,n)n<h(m,n).

## Lemma 4.8.

LetA⊆P^A\subseteq\widehat{P}be a subset such that every finite subset ofAAhas an upper bound. ThenAAmeet at most 3 different sub-posetsLnL_{n}.

Suppose4≤|M|4\leq|M|and take(n¯i,t¯i)∈A(\overline{n}_{i},\overline{t}_{i})\in A,i=1,2,3,4i=1,2,3,4with{n¯1,n¯2,n¯3,n¯4}⊆M\{\overline{n}_{1},\overline{n}_{2},\overline{n}_{3},\overline{n}_{4}\}\subseteq M. Then{(n¯1,t¯1),(n¯2,t¯2),(n¯3,t¯3),(n¯4,t¯4)}\{(\overline{n}_{1},\overline{t}_{1}),(\overline{n}_{2},\overline{t}_{2}),(\overline{n}_{3},\overline{t}_{3}),(\overline{n}_{4},\overline{t}_{4})\}has an upper boundaua^{u}. Thereforeau=(fa⋄,b⋄​(s⋄),⊤)a^{u}=(f_{a^{\diamond},b^{\diamond}}(s^{\diamond}),\top)for somes∈ℕ<ωs\in\mathbb{N}^{<\omega}anda⋄,b⋄∈ℕa^{\diamond},b^{\diamond}\in\mathbb{N}. For convenience, lets⋄=a1′​a2′​⋯​an⋄′s^{\diamond}=a^{\prime}_{1}a^{\prime}_{2}\cdot\cdot\cdot a^{\prime}_{n^{\diamond}}. Ifl​e​n​(s⋄)=1len(s^{\diamond})=1, then↓​au=↓​(fa⋄,b⋄​(a1′),⊤)=Lfa⋄,b⋄​(a1′)∪{(a,a1′)}∪{(b,a)∣1≤a≤a1′}\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003pta^{u}=\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003pt(f_{a^{\diamond},b^{\diamond}}(a^{\prime}_{1}),\top)=L_{f_{a^{\diamond},b^{\diamond}}(a^{\prime}_{1})}\cup\{(a,a^{\prime}_{1})\}\cup\{(b,a)\mid 1\leq a\leq a^{\prime}_{1}\}. Now, we can see the first projection of↓​au\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003pta^{u}has 3 natural numbers, a contradiction. If1<l​e​n​(s⋄)1<len(s^{\diamond}), then↓​au=↓​(fa⋄,b⋄​(a1′​a2′​⋯​an⋄′),⊤)=Lfa⋄,b⋄​(a1′​a2′​⋯​an⋄′)∪{(a,a1′​a2′​⋯​am⋄′)∣1≤m⋄≤n⋄}∪{(fa⋄,b⋄​(a1′​a2′​⋯​an⋄−1′),a)∣1≤a≤an⋄′}\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003pta^{u}=\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003pt(f_{a^{\diamond},b^{\diamond}}(a^{\prime}_{1}a^{\prime}_{2}\cdot\cdot\cdot a^{\prime}_{n^{\diamond}}),\top)=L_{f_{a^{\diamond},b^{\diamond}}(a^{\prime}_{1}a^{\prime}_{2}\cdot\cdot\cdot a^{\prime}_{n^{\diamond}})}\cup\{(a,a^{\prime}_{1}a^{\prime}_{2}\cdot\cdot\cdot a^{\prime}_{m^{\diamond}})\mid 1\leq m^{\diamond}\leq n^{\diamond}\}\cup\{(f_{a^{\diamond},b^{\diamond}}(a^{\prime}_{1}a^{\prime}_{2}\cdot\cdot\cdot a^{\prime}_{n^{\diamond}-1}),a)\mid 1\leq a\leq a^{\prime}_{n^{\diamond}}\}. ## Proposition 4.11.

By Theorem 4.3, and Corollary 4.4, we have(P^,ω​(P^))(\widehat{P},\omega(\widehat{P}))is notS​ISI-compact andΣ​ω​(P^)\Sigma\omega(\widehat{P})is non sober.

Note thatω​(P^)={P^∖⋂i∈I↑​Fi∣(Fi)i∈I​is a family of finite subsets of​P^}.\omega(\widehat{P})=\{\widehat{P}\setminus\bigcap\limits_{i\in I}\mathord{\uparrow}\hskip 0.50003ptF_{i}\mid(F_{i})_{i\in I}\ \mbox{is a family of finite subsets of}\ \widehat{P}\}.SinceP^\widehat{P}is countable, the set of all finite subsets ofP^\widehat{P}is still countable. Lets=(sn)n∈ℕ,t=(tn)n∈ℕ∈{1}×ℕ<ωs=(s_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},t=(t_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in\{1\}\times\mathbb{N}^{<\omega}be sequences such thatssis increasing and

In the following, we present a countable compactT1T_{1}space which is nonS​ISI-compact.

## Lemma 4.12.

Let(X,τ)(X,\tau)be a space which is notS​ISI-compact. Ifτ^\widehat{\tau}is a topology onXXsuch thatτ⊆τ^\tau\subseteq\widehat{\tau}, then(X,τ^)(X,\widehat{\tau})is notS​ISI-compact.

## Proof.

Consider the inclusion mapi:τ⟶τ^i:\tau\longrightarrow\widehat{\tau}. Clearly,iiis Scott continuous. Since(X,τ)(X,\tau)is a space which is notS​ISI-compact, there is an open cover𝒰⊆τ∖{X}\mathcal{U}\subseteq\tau\setminus\{X\}ofXXwhich is irreducible inΣ​τ\Sigma\tau. Hence,i​(𝒰)=𝒰i(\mathcal{U})=\mathcal{U}is still irreducible inΣ​(τ^)\Sigma(\hat{\tau}). Thus,(X,τ^)(X,\widehat{\tau})is notS​ISI-compact.∎

## Corollary 4.13.

There is a countableT1T_{1}spaceXXwhich is compact but notS​ISI-compact.

## Proof.

LetX=(P^,ω​(P^))X=(\widehat{P},\omega(\widehat{P})). Consider the topologyτ^\widehat{\tau}which has𝒜=τ∪{X∖F∣F​is a finite subset of​X}\mathcal{A}=\tau\cup\{X\setminus F\mid F\ \mbox{is a finite subset of}\ X\}

as a subbase, whereτ=ω​(P^)\tau=\omega(\widehat{P}). Obviously,(X,τ^)(X,\widehat{\tau})isT1T_{1}. Let𝒰⊆𝒜\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{A}be an cover ofXX. We consider the following two cases:

(1) There is a finite subsetF⊆XF\subseteq Xsuch thatX∖F∈𝒰X\setminus F\in\mathcal{U}.

For eachx∈Fx\in F, take aUx∈𝒰U_{x}\in\mathcal{U}. We see that𝒱⊆𝒰\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{U}is a finite subcover ofXX, where𝒱={X∖F}∪{Ux∣x∈F}\mathcal{V}=\{X\setminus F\}\cup\{U_{x}\mid x\in F\}.

(2)𝒰∩{X∖F∣F​is a finite subset of​X}=∅\mathcal{U}\cap\{X\setminus F\mid F\ \mbox{is a finite subset of}\ X\}=\emptyset.

In this case, we have𝒰⊆ω​(P^)\mathcal{U}\subseteq\omega(\widehat{P}). Since(X,ω​(P^))(X,\omega(\widehat{P}))is a compact space, there is a finite subset𝒲⊆𝒰\mathcal{W}\subseteq\mathcal{U}such that𝒲\mathcal{W}coversXX.

Therefore,(X,τ^)(X,\widehat{\tau})is compact. By Lemma 4.12,(X,τ^)(X,\widehat{\tau})is notS​ISI-compact.∎

We have seen that compactness andS​ISI-compactness are not equivalent in upper topology, lower topology of posets. The following Proposition 4.14 states that they are equivalent for the Scott topology of posets.

## Proposition 4.14.

LetLLbe a poset. The following statements are equivalent.

(1)Σ​L\Sigma Lis compact;

(2)L=↑​FL=\mathord{\uparrow}\hskip 0.50003ptFfor some nonempty finite subsetF⊆LF\subseteq L;

(3)Σ​L\Sigma LisS​ISI-compact.

(4)Σ​L\Sigma Lisυ\upsilon-compact.

## Proof.

By Corollary 3.14, it is enough to prove that (2) implies (4).

By (2), we can further require thatF=m​i​n​(L)F=min(L). Assume thatF={x0,x1,⋯,xn}.F=\{x_{0},x_{1},\cdot\cdot\cdot,x_{n}\}.

Claim 1:L∖{xi}∈σ​(L)L\setminus\{x_{i}\}\in\sigma(L)for all0≤i≤n0\leq i\leq n.

Obviously,L∖{xi}L\setminus\{x_{i}\}is an upper set sincexix_{i}is a minimal element. LetDDbe a directed set such that⋁D\bigvee Dexists and⋁D∈L∖{xi}\bigvee D\in L\setminus\{x_{i}\}. IfD∩L∖{xi}=∅D\cap L\setminus\{x_{i}\}=\emptyset, thenD={xi}D=\{x_{i}\}, a contradiction. So we haveD∩L∖{xi}≠∅D\cap L\setminus\{x_{i}\}\neq\emptyset. This shows thatL∖{xi}∈σ​(L)L\setminus\{x_{i}\}\in\sigma(L).

Let𝒰⊆σ​(L)\mathcal{U}\subseteq\sigma(L)be an irreducible open cover ofLLin the upper topology ofσ​(L)\sigma(L). Assume thatL∉𝒰L\not\in\mathcal{U}. Then we have𝒰⊆⋃0≤i≤n↓σ​(L)​L∖{xi}.\mathcal{U}\subseteq\bigcup\limits_{0\leq i\leq n}\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003pt_{\sigma(L)}L\setminus\{x_{i}\}.

By the irreducibility of𝒰\mathcal{U},𝒰⊆↓σ​(L)​L∖{xi0}\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003pt_{\sigma(L)}L\setminus\{x_{i_{0}}\}for some0≤i0≤n0\leq i_{0}\leq n. This means that⋃𝒰=L∖{xi0}\bigcup\mathcal{U}=L\setminus\{x_{i_{0}}\}, a contradiction. HenceX∈𝒰X\in\mathcal{U}and thusΣ​L\Sigma Lisυ\upsilon-compact.
∎

## 5Weakυ\upsilon-compactness

By the proof of Proposition 3.20, for a infiniteT1T_{1}spaceXX, if an open cover𝒰\mathcal{U}withoutXXis irreducible in(𝒪(X),υ(𝒪(X))(\mathcal{O}(X),\upsilon(\mathcal{O}(X)), then𝒰\mathcal{U}does not contain infinitely many open sets coming fromℱ​(X)\mathscr{F}(X). So we modify the definition ofυ\upsilon-compactness.

## Definition 5.1.

A topological spaceXXis called weaklyυ\upsilon-compact if for every irreducible open cover𝒰\mathcal{U}ofXXin the upper topology of𝒪​(X)\mathcal{O}(X)with𝒰∩ℱ​(X)\mathcal{U}\cap\mathscr{F}(X)being finite,X∈𝒰X\in\mathcal{U}.

## Proposition 5.2.

Every weaklyυ\upsilon-compact space is compact.

## Proof.

Let𝒰⊆𝒪​(X)\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(X)be a directed open cover ofXX. Then𝒰\mathcal{U}is irreducible in the upper topology of𝒪​(X)\mathcal{O}(X). If𝒰∩ℱ​(X)≠∅\mathcal{U}\cap\mathscr{F}(X)\neq\emptyset, then by the directedness of𝒰\mathcal{U}, it is not difficult to show thatX∈𝒰X\in\mathcal{U}. If on the contrary,𝒰∩ℱ​(X)=∅\mathcal{U}\cap\mathscr{F}(X)=\emptyset, then by the weakυ\upsilon-compactness ofXX,X∈𝒰X\in\mathcal{U}. Therefore,XXis compact.∎

## Example 5.3.

(1) Equip the natural numbers set𝐍\mathbf{N}with the co-finite topologyτc​o​f\tau_{cof}. Then(𝐍,τc​o​f)(\mathbf{N},\tau_{cof})is weaklyυ\upsilon-compact but notυ\upsilon-compact. Similarly, the topological spaceYYgiven in Example 3.19 is weaklyυ\upsilon-compact.

(2) LetXXbe an uncountable infinite set anda∈Xa\in X. The topologyτ\tauonXXis given byτ={X∖F∣F​is a finite set of​X}∪{V⊆X∣a∉V}.\tau=\{X\setminus F\mid F\ \mbox{is a finite set of}\ X\}\cup\{V\subseteq X\mid a\not\in V\}.

ThenXXis a compact Hausdorff space but not first-countable. By Proposition 3.20,(X,τ)(X,\tau)is notυ\upsilon-compact. Let𝒰⊆τ\mathcal{U}\subseteq\taube an irreducible cover ofXXin the upper topology of𝒪​(X)\mathcal{O}(X)with𝒰∩ℱ​(X)\mathcal{U}\cap\mathscr{F}(X)being finite. Assume thatX∉𝒰X\not\in\mathcal{U}. Naturally the following question arises.

∙\bulletLetX⊆𝔼X\subseteq\mathbb{E}be a compact subspace. When isXXweaklyυ\upsilon-compact?

In the following, we will completely answer that question. Given a subsetM⊆ℝmM\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}andx∈Mx\in M,xxis a limit point inMMif there is a strictly monotone sequence(xn)n∈ℕ⊆M∖{x}(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq M\setminus\{x\}such thatlimn⟶∞xn=x\lim\limits_{n\longrightarrow\infty}x_{n}=x. We usel​i​m​(M)lim(M)to denote the set of all limit-points inMM.

## Theorem 5.4.

LetX⊆𝔼X\subseteq\mathbb{E}be a compact subspace of the real line. Then the following statements are equivalent.

(a)XXis weaklyυ\upsilon-compact;

Furthermore, upon possibly truncating the sequences(fmn)m∈ℕ(f^{n}_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}, we can assume that for alln∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N},

(1)(fmn)m∈ℕ⊆(−∞,kn)(f^{n}_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq(-\infty,k_{n})or(fmn)m∈ℕ⊆(kn,+∞)(f^{n}_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq(k_{n},+\infty);

and⋃𝒰n=X∖{kn+1,kn+2},if​(fmn+1)m∈ℕ⊆(kn+1,+∞).\bigcup\mathcal{U}_{n}=X\setminus\{k_{n+1},k_{n+2}\},\ \mbox{if}\ (f^{n+1}_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq(k_{n+1},+\infty).

Based on this consideration, we can require{a1,a2,⋯,as}⊆(−∞,k)\{a_{1},a_{2},\cdot\cdot\cdot,a_{s}\}\subseteq(-\infty,k). By Claim 1 andX∉𝒰X\not\in\mathcal{U}, we know thatXXis not weaklyυ\upsilon-compact, a contradiction. Therefore,l​i​m​(X)lim(X)is finite.

(b)⟹(a){\rm(b)\Longrightarrow(a)}Let𝒱⊆𝒪​(X)\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(X)be an irreducible cover ofXXwith respect to the upper topology of𝒪​(X)\mathcal{O}(X)and𝒱∩ℱ​(X)\mathcal{V}\cap\mathscr{F}(X)is finite. Assume that there is aW∈𝒰W\in\mathcal{U}such thatW∉ℱ​(X)W\not\in\mathscr{F}(X). Since{x0,x1,⋯,xm}⊆W∈𝒪​(X)\{x_{0},x_{1},\cdot\cdot\cdot,x_{m}\}\subseteq W\in\mathcal{O}(X), there are finitely many open intervals{(ak,bk)∣ak<bk,k=0,1,⋯,m}\{(a_{k},b_{k})\mid a_{k}<b_{k},k=0,1,\cdot\cdot\cdot,m\}

such thatxk∈(ak,bk)∩X⊆Wx_{k}\in(a_{k},b_{k})\cap X\subseteq Wfor all0≤k≤m0\leq k\leq m. Thencn<cc_{n}<c(orc<cnc<c_{n}) for alln∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N}andc∈Xc\in XsinceXXis closed. So we havec∈l​i​m​(X)c\in lim(X)and hencec=xsc=x_{s}for some1≤s≤m1\leq s\leq m. By Claim 2,𝒰\mathcal{U}is finite because𝒱∩ℱ​(X)\mathcal{V}\cap\mathscr{F}(X)is finite. IfX∉𝒱X\not\in\mathcal{V}, we can conclude that𝒱⊆↓𝒪​(X)​{X∖{x0},X∖{x1},⋯,X∖{xn}}∪↓𝒪​(X)​𝒰.\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003pt_{\mathcal{O}(X)}\{X\setminus\{x_{0}\},X\setminus\{x_{1}\},\cdot\cdot\cdot,X\setminus\{x_{n}\}\}\cup\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003pt_{\mathcal{O}(X)}\mathcal{U}.

## Remark 5.5.

Theorem 5.4 is still valid for the higher dimension spaceℝn\mathbb{R}^{n}. Specifically, a compact subsetM⊆ℝnM\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}is weaklyυ\upsilon-compact iffl​i​m​(M)lim(M)is finite.

## Example 5.6.

LetXXbe the topological space given in Example 5.3(4). ThenXXis weaklyυ\upsilon-compact. ButX×X⊆ℝ2X\times X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}has infinitely limit points. HenceX×XX\times Xis not weaklyυ\upsilon-compact.

The Sorgenfrey lineℝr\mathbb{R}_{r}is a topological space onℝ\mathbb{R}having a base{[x,y)∣x<y}\{[x,y)\mid x<y\}. Dually, we equipℝ\mathbb{R}with the topology having a base{(x,y]∣x<y}\{(x,y]\mid x<y\}and the corresponding topological space is denoted byℝl\mathbb{R}_{l}.

## Lemma 5.7.

(see[24]) A subsetAAofℝ\mathbb{R}is compact in Sorgenfrey line iffA⊆ℝA\subseteq\mathbb{R}is bounded and closed in real lineℝ\mathbb{R}andAAhas no accumulation points inℝl\mathbb{R}_{l}.

Given a subsetA⊆ℝA\subseteq\mathbb{R},xxis a left(right) limit point ofAAif there is a strictly increasing (decreasing) sequence(xn)n∈ℕ⊆A∖{x}(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq A\setminus\{x\}such thatlimn⟶∞xn=x\lim\limits_{n\longrightarrow\infty}x_{n}=xin real lineℝ\mathbb{R}. The set of all left(right) limit points ofAAis denoted byl​(A)l(A)(r​(A)r(A)).

## Proposition 5.8.

LetA⊆ℝA\subseteq\mathbb{R}. Thenxxis an accumulation point ofAAinℝl\mathbb{R}_{l}iffx∈l​(A)x\in l(A). Dually,xxis an accumulation point ofAAinℝr\mathbb{R}_{r}iffx∈r​(A)x\in r(A).

## Proof.

Supposexxis an accumulation point ofAAinℝl\mathbb{R}_{l}. Then for every open neighbourhoodUUofxxwhich is open in the subspaceAAofℝl\mathbb{R}_{l},U∩(A∖{x})≠∅U\cap(A\setminus\{x\})\neq\emptyset.

Assuming thatUn=(x−1n,x]∩(xn−1]∩AU_{n}=(x-\frac{1}{n},x]\cap(x_{n-1}]\cap Aandx1,⋯,xnx_{1},\cdot\cdot\cdot,x_{n}are defined, we setUn+1=(x−1n+1,x]∩(xn,x]∩AU_{n+1}=(x-\frac{1}{n+1},x]\cap(x_{n},x]\cap Aand take forxn+1x_{n+1}any element ofUn+1∩(A∖{x})U_{n+1}\cap(A\setminus\{x\})which is nonempty by our assumptionUnU_{n}is open in the subspaceAAofℝl\mathbb{R}_{l}.

Then, we obtain a sequence(xn)n∈ℕ⊆A(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq Asuch that for alln∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N},xn<xn+1x_{n}<x_{n+1}andx−1n+1<xn+1<xx-\frac{1}{n+1}<x_{n+1}<x. Then(xn)n∈ℕ⊆A(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq Ais strictly increasing andlimn⟶∞xn=x\lim\limits_{n\longrightarrow\infty}x_{n}=x. Equivalently,x∈l​(A)x\in l(A).

Conversely,x∈l​(A)x\in l(A)implies that there is a strictly increasing sequence(an)n∈ℕ⊆A∖{x}(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq A\setminus\{x\}such thatlimn⟶∞an=x\lim\limits_{n\longrightarrow\infty}a_{n}=x. Ifa<xa<x, then there is aN∈ℕN\in\mathbb{N}such that|aN−x|<x−a|a_{N}-x|<x-a. This impliesaN∈(a,x]∩A∖{x}a_{N}\in(a,x]\cap A\setminus\{x\}. Hence,xxis an accumulation point ofAA. The proof of the second statement is similar.∎

## 6The𝒯\mathcal{T}-compactness of Subspaces, product spaces and power spaces

In this section, we discuss the properties ofS​ISI-compactness,υ\upsilon-compactness and weakυ\upsilon-compactness. Especially, the heredity for closed subspaces of those kinds of compactness is obtained.

## Theorem 6.1.

Every topological space(X,τ)(X,\tau)is an open subspace of aυ\upsilon-compact space.

## Proof.

LetYYbe a set withX⊆YX\subseteq YandY∖X≠∅Y\setminus X\neq\emptyset. EquipY∖XY\setminus Xwith a topologyσ\sigmasuch that(Y∖X,σ)(Y\setminus X,\sigma)isυ\upsilon-compact. Putρ=τ∪{X∪U∣U∈σ}\rho=\tau\cup\{X\cup U\mid U\in\sigma\}. It is easy to show thatρ\rhois a topology onYYandXXis a dense open subspace of(Y,ρ)(Y,\rho). Define a mapψ:ρ⟶σ\psi:\rho\longrightarrow\sigmaby∀W∈ρ,ψ​(W)=W∖X.\forall\ W\in\rho,\ \ \psi(W)=W\setminus X.

Then for everyV∈σV\in\sigma,ψ−1​(σ∖↓​V)=ρ∖↓​(X∪V)\psi^{-1}(\sigma\setminus\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003ptV)=\rho\setminus\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003pt(X\cup V). It follows thatψ\psiis continuous with respect to the upper topology. Let𝒰⊆ρ\mathcal{U}\subseteq\rhobe an irreducible open cover ofYYin the upper topology of𝒪​(Y)\mathcal{O}(Y). By the continuity ofψ\psi,ψ​(𝒰)\psi(\mathcal{U})is irreducible in the upper topology of𝒪​(Y∖X)\mathcal{O}(Y\setminus X)andψ​(𝒰)\psi(\mathcal{U})coversY∖XY\setminus X. Since(Y∖X,σ)(Y\setminus X,\sigma)isυ\upsilon-compact, there is aU∈𝒰U\in\mathcal{U}such thatψ​(U)=Y∖X\psi(U)=Y\setminus X. So we can conclude thatU=YU=Yand hence(Y,ρ)(Y,\rho)isυ\upsilon-compact.∎

## Theorem 6.2.

Every topological space(X,τ)(X,\tau)is an open subspace of a weaklyυ\upsilon-compact space.

## Proof.

It is similar to the proof of Theorem 6.1.∎

## Theorem 6.3.

Let(X,τ)(X,\tau)be a weaklyυ\upsilon-compact topological space andY⊆XY\subseteq Xa closed set. Then the subspaceYYis weaklyυ\upsilon-compact.

## Proof.

It is clear whenYYis finite, so assume this is not the case. Let𝒱⊆τ|Y\mathcal{V}\subseteq\tau|_{Y}be an irreducible cover ofYYwith respect to the upper topology of𝒪​(Y)\mathcal{O}(Y)and𝒱∩ℱ​(Y)\mathcal{V}\cap\mathscr{F}(Y)is finite. Define a mapg:𝒪​(Y)⟶𝒪​(X)g:\mathcal{O}(Y)\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}(X)by∀U∈𝒪​(Y),g​(U)=(X∖Y)∪(⋃{V∈𝒪​(X)∣V∩Y=U}).\forall\ U\in\mathcal{O}(Y),\ g(U)=(X\setminus Y)\cup(\bigcup\{V\in\mathcal{O}(X)\mid V\cap Y=U\}).

Take aW∈𝒪​(X)W\in\mathcal{O}(X),g−1​(↓𝒪​(X)​W)={G∈𝒪​(Y)∣g​(G)⊆W}g^{-1}(\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003pt_{\mathcal{O}(X)}W)=\{G\in\mathcal{O}(Y)\mid g(G)\subseteq W\}. Obviously,g−1​(↓𝒪​(X)​W)=∅g^{-1}(\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003pt_{\mathcal{O}(X)}W)=\emptysetifX∖Y⊈WX\setminus Y\not\subseteq W. IfX∖Y⊆WX\setminus Y\subseteq W, we can verifyg−1​(↓𝒪​(X)​W)=↓𝒪​(Y)​(W∩Y)g^{-1}(\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003pt_{\mathcal{O}(X)}W)=\mathord{\downarrow}\hskip 0.50003pt_{\mathcal{O}(Y)}(W\cap Y). This means thatggis continuous with respect to the upper topology on𝒪​(X)\mathcal{O}(X)and𝒪​(Y)\mathcal{O}(Y). Theng​(𝒱)g(\mathcal{V})is still irreducible in the upper topology of𝒪​(X)\mathcal{O}(X)andg​(𝒱)g(\mathcal{V})covesXX. In addition, asYYand𝒱∩ℱ​(Y)\mathcal{V}\cap\mathscr{F}(Y)are infinite,g​(𝒱)∩ℱ​(X)g(\mathcal{V})\cap\mathscr{F}(X)is finite. By the weakυ\upsilon-compactness ofXX,X∈g​(𝒱)X\in g(\mathcal{V}). Whence,X=g​(V0)=⋃𝒟X=g(V_{0})=\bigcup\mathcal{D}for someV0∈𝒱V_{0}\in\mathcal{V}, where𝒟={(X∖Y)∪H∣H∩Y=V0,H∈𝒪​(X)}.\mathcal{D}=\{(X\setminus Y)\cup H\mid H\cap Y=V_{0},H\in\mathcal{O}(X)\}.

By Theorem 6.1,XXis compact. Since𝒟\mathcal{D}is directed andX=⋃𝒟X=\bigcup\mathcal{D},X∈𝒟X\in\mathcal{D}. Equivalently,Y=V0∈𝒱Y=V_{0}\in\mathcal{V}. Therefore,YYis weaklyυ\upsilon-compact.∎

## Theorem 6.4.

Let(X,τ)(X,\tau)be aυ\upsilon-compact topological space andY⊆XY\subseteq Xa closed set. Then the subspaceYYisυ\upsilon-compact.

## Proof.

It is similar to the proof of Theorem 6.3.∎

## Proposition 6.5.

Let(X,τ)(X,\tau)be a topological space andY⊆XY\subseteq Xsuch that{X}={U∈τ∣Y⊆U}\{X\}=\{U\in\tau\mid Y\subseteq U\}. The following statements hold:

(1)the subspaceYYis compact iffXXis compact;

(2)if the subspaceYYis SI-compact, thenXXisS​ISI-compact.

## Proof.

(1) Assume first thatYYis compact. Let𝒱⊆τ\mathcal{V}\subseteq\taube a cover ofXX. Then𝒱\mathcal{V}coversYYand hence by the compactness ofYY, there are finitelyV1,V2,⋯,Vn∈𝒱V_{1},V_{2},\cdot\cdot\cdot,V_{n}\in\mathcal{V}such thatY⊆⋃1≤s≤nVsY\subseteq\bigcup\limits_{1\leq s\leq n}V_{s}. By assumption,X=⋃1≤s≤nVsX=\bigcup\limits_{1\leq s\leq n}V_{s}. SoXXis compact.

Conversely, let𝒰\mathcal{U}be an open cover ofYY. By assumption,X=⋃𝒰X=\bigcup\mathcal{U}. SinceXXis compact, there areU1,U2,…,Um∈𝒰U_{1},U_{2},...,U_{m}\in\mathcal{U}such thatX=⋃1≤t≤mUtX=\bigcup\limits_{1\leq t\leq m}U_{t}. This means thatYYis compact.

(2) Let𝒰={Ui∣Ui∈τ,i∈I}\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\mid U_{i}\in\tau,i\in I\}be a Scott irreducible open cover ofXX. Then𝒰^={Ui∩Y∣i∈I}\hat{\mathcal{U}}=\{U_{i}\cap Y\mid i\in I\}coversYY. Define a mapfY:Σ​(τ)⟶Σ​(τ|Y)f_{Y}:\Sigma(\tau)\longrightarrow\Sigma(\tau|_{Y})by∀V∈τ,fY​(V)=Y∩V.\forall\ V\in\tau,f_{Y}(V)=Y\cap V.

Clearly,fYf_{Y}is a continuous map sincefYf_{Y}preserves suprema of directed sets. Then𝒰^=f​(𝒰)\hat{\mathcal{U}}=f(\mathcal{U})is also irreducible inΣ​(τ|Y)\Sigma(\tau|_{Y}). It follows from the SI-compactness ofYYthatY∈𝒰^Y\in\hat{\mathcal{U}}. This means thatA⊆UiA\subseteq U_{i}fori∈Ii\in I. By the assumption,X=Ui∈𝒰X=U_{i}\in\mathcal{U}. Therefore,XXisS​ISI-compact.∎

## Theorem 6.6.

Every space(X,τ)(X,\tau)is an open subspace of a SI-compact space.

## Proof.

LetYYbe a set andX⊆YX\subseteq YwithY∖X≠∅Y\setminus X\neq\emptyset. EquipY∖XY\setminus Xwith a topologyδ\deltasuch that(Y∖X,δ)(Y\setminus X,\delta)is SI-compact. Setω=τ∪{X∪U∣U∈δ}\omega=\tau\cup\{X\cup U\mid U\in\delta\}. It is easy to show thatω\omegais a topology onYYandXXis a dense open subspace of(Y,ω)(Y,\omega). Define a mapφ:ω⟶δ\varphi:\omega\longrightarrow\deltaby∀W∈ω,φ​(W)=W∖X.\forall\ W\in\omega,\ \ \varphi(W)=W\setminus X.

Thenφ\varphiis continuous with respect to the Scott topology. Let𝒰⊆ω\mathcal{U}\subseteq\omegabe a Scott irreducible open cover ofYY. By the continuity ofφ\varphi,φ​(𝒰)\varphi(\mathcal{U})is Scott irreducible andφ​(𝒰)\varphi(\mathcal{U})coversY∖XY\setminus X. Since(Y∖X,δ)(Y\setminus X,\delta)is SI-compact, there is aU∈𝒰U\in\mathcal{U}such thatφ​(U)=Y∖X\varphi(U)=Y\setminus X. So we can conclude thatU=YU=Yand hence(Y,ω)(Y,\omega)is SI-compact.∎

## Remark 6.7.

By Theorem 6.6, open subspaces of aS​ISI-compact space are notS​ISI-compact in general.

## Theorem 6.8.

Let(X,τ)(X,\tau)be aS​ISI-compact topological space andY⊆XY\subseteq Xa closed set. Then the subspaceYYisS​ISI-compact.

Actually, ifx∈g​(⋃i∈IUi)x\in g(\bigcup\limits_{i\in I}U_{i}), thenx∈V∈τx\in V\in\tauwithV∩Y=⋃i∈IUiV\cap Y=\bigcup\limits_{i\in I}U_{i}orx∈X∖Yx\in X\setminus Y. It follows that there is aW∈τW\in\tauwithW∩Y=Ui1W\cap Y=U_{i_{1}}such thaty∈Wy\in W. As{Ui∣i∈I}⊆τ|Y\{U_{i}\mid i\in I\}\subseteq\tau|_{Y}, we can choose for eachj∈I∖{i1}j\in I\setminus\{i_{1}\}, aWj∈τW_{j}\in\tausatisfyingWj∩Y=UjW_{j}\cap Y=U_{j}. By this Claim,ggis continuous with respect to the Scott topology. So we knowg​(𝒰)g(\mathcal{U})is Scott irreducible inΣ​(τ)\Sigma(\tau). Furthermore,g​(𝒰)g(\mathcal{U})coversXX. By theS​ISI-compactness ofXX,X∈g​(𝒰)X\in g(\mathcal{U}). WhenceX=g​(U0)=⋃𝒟X=g(U_{0})=\bigcup\mathcal{D}, where𝒟={(X∖Y)∪V∣V∈τ,V∩Y=U0}\mathcal{D}=\{(X\setminus Y)\cup V\mid V\in\tau,V\cap Y=U_{0}\}

for someU0∈𝒰U_{0}\in\mathcal{U}. Since𝒟\mathcal{D}is directed,X∈𝒟X\in\mathcal{D}by using theS​ISI-compactness ofXXagain. ThusU0=Y∈𝒰U_{0}=Y\in\mathcal{U}and thenYYisS​ISI-compact.∎

## Definition 6.9.

A topological spaceXXis called weaklyS​ISI-compact if there is a baseℬ\mathcal{B}of the topology ofXXsuch that a Scott irreducible open cover𝒰\mathcal{U}ofXXcontainsXXas soon as𝒰\mathcal{U}is contained inℬ\mathcal{B}.

## Theorem 6.10.

LetXXandYYbe compact spaces. IfΣ​𝒪​(X)\Sigma\mathcal{O}(X)andΣ​𝒪​(Y)\Sigma\mathcal{O}(Y)are sober, thenX×YX\times Yis weaklyS​ISI-compact.

## Proof.

Define a mapf:Σ​𝒪​(X×Y)⟶Σ​𝒪​(X)×Σ​𝒪​(Y)f:\Sigma\mathcal{O}(X\times Y)\longrightarrow\Sigma\mathcal{O}(X)\times\Sigma\mathcal{O}(Y)as follows:∀M∈𝒪​(X×Y),f​(M)=(p​rX​(M),p​rY​(M)),\forall\ M\in\mathcal{O}(X\times Y),f(M)=(pr_{X}(M),pr_{Y}(M)),

wherep​rX,p​rYpr_{X},pr_{Y}are projections.

Claim :ffis continuous.

Let𝒜∈σ​(𝒪​(X))\mathcal{A}\in\sigma(\mathcal{O}(X))andℬ∈σ​(𝒪​(X))\mathcal{B}\in\sigma(\mathcal{O}(X)). There is no doubt thatf−1​(𝒜×ℬ)f^{-1}(\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B})is an upper set. If𝒟\mathcal{D}is a directed subset of𝒪​(X×Y)\mathcal{O}(X\times Y)with⋃𝒟∈f−1​(𝒜×ℬ)\bigcup\mathcal{D}\in f^{-1}(\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}), then⋃p​rX​(𝒟)∈𝒜\bigcup pr_{X}(\mathcal{D})\in\mathcal{A}and⋃p​rY​(𝒟)∈ℬ\bigcup pr_{Y}(\mathcal{D})\in\mathcal{B}. Since𝒜\mathcal{A}andℬ\mathcal{B}are Scott open, there areD1,D2∈𝒟D_{1},D_{2}\in\mathcal{D}such thatp​rX​(D1)∈𝒜pr_{X}(D_{1})\in\mathcal{A}andp​rY​(D2)∈ℬpr_{Y}(D_{2})\in\mathcal{B}. Using the directedness of𝒟\mathcal{D}, take aD3∈𝒟D_{3}\in\mathcal{D}withD1∪D2⊆D3D_{1}\cup D_{2}\subseteq D_{3}. Then we knowD3∈𝒟∩f−1​(𝒜×ℬ)D_{3}\in\mathcal{D}\cap f^{-1}(\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}).

Consider𝒮={U×V∣U∈𝒪​(X),V∈𝒪​(Y)}\mathcal{S}=\{\ U\times V\mid U\in\mathcal{O}(X),V\in\mathcal{O}(Y)\}. Then𝒮\mathcal{S}is a base ofX×YX\times Y. Let𝒰⊆𝒮\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{S}be a Scott irreducible open cover ofX×YX\times Y. Assume thatX×Y∉𝒰X\times Y\not\in\mathcal{U}. By Claim,f​(𝒰)f(\mathcal{U})is irreducible in the product spaceΣ​𝒪​(X)×Σ​𝒪​(Y)\Sigma\mathcal{O}(X)\times\Sigma\mathcal{O}(Y). AsΣ​𝒪​(X)\Sigma\mathcal{O}(X)andΣ​𝒪​(Y)\Sigma\mathcal{O}(Y)are sober,Σ​𝒪​(X)×Σ​𝒪​(Y)\Sigma\mathcal{O}(X)\times\Sigma\mathcal{O}(Y)is also sober. So there areM∈𝒪​(X)M\in\mathcal{O}(X)andN∈𝒪​(Y)N\in\mathcal{O}(Y)such thatclΣ​𝒪​(X)×Σ​𝒪​(Y)(f(𝒰))=↓𝒪​(X)×O​(Y)(M,N)=↓𝒪​(X)M×↓𝒪​(Y)N.cl_{\Sigma\mathcal{O}(X)\times\Sigma\mathcal{O}(Y)}(f(\mathcal{U}))=\downarrow_{\mathcal{O}(X)\times{O}(Y)}(M,N)=\downarrow_{\mathcal{O}(X)}M\times\downarrow_{\mathcal{O}(Y)}N.

Then we havef(𝒰)⊆↓𝒪​(X)M×↓𝒪​(Y)Nf(\mathcal{U})\subseteq\downarrow_{\mathcal{O}(X)}M\times\downarrow_{\mathcal{O}(Y)}N. This means that⋃p​rX​(𝒰)⊆M\bigcup pr_{X}(\mathcal{U})\subseteq Mand⋃p​rY​(𝒰)⊆N\bigcup pr_{Y}(\mathcal{U})\subseteq N. Equivalently,X=p​rX​(⋃𝒰)=MX=pr_{X}(\bigcup\mathcal{U})=MandY=p​rY​(⋃𝒰)=NY=pr_{Y}(\bigcup\mathcal{U})=N. SinceXXandYYare compact,𝒪​(X)∖{X}\mathcal{O}(X)\setminus\{X\}and𝒪​(Y)∖{Y}\mathcal{O}(Y)\setminus\{Y\}are Scott closed inΣ​𝒪​(X)\Sigma\mathcal{O}(X)andΣ​𝒪​(Y)\Sigma\mathcal{O}(Y), respectively. Note that𝒪​(X)×𝒪​(Y)∖{(X,Y)}=(𝒪​(X)∖{X}×𝒪​(Y))∪(𝒪​(X)×𝒪​(Y)∖{Y}).\mathcal{O}(X)\times\mathcal{O}(Y)\setminus\{(X,Y)\}=(\mathcal{O}(X)\setminus\{X\}\times\mathcal{O}(Y))\cup(\mathcal{O}(X)\times\mathcal{O}(Y)\setminus\{Y\}).

It follows that𝒪​(X)×𝒪​(Y)∖{(X,Y)}\mathcal{O}(X)\times\mathcal{O}(Y)\setminus\{(X,Y)\}is closed in the product spaceΣ​𝒪​(X)×Σ​𝒪​(Y)\Sigma\mathcal{O}(X)\times\Sigma\mathcal{O}(Y). Clearly,f​(𝒰)⊆𝒪​(X)×𝒪​(Y)∖{(X,Y)}f(\mathcal{U})\subseteq\mathcal{O}(X)\times\mathcal{O}(Y)\setminus\{(X,Y)\}and thenc​lΣ​𝒪​(X)×Σ​𝒪​(Y)​(f​(𝒰))=𝒪​(X)×𝒪​(Y)⊆𝒪​(X)×𝒪​(Y)∖{(X,Y)},cl_{\Sigma\mathcal{O}(X)\times\Sigma\mathcal{O}(Y)}(f(\mathcal{U}))=\mathcal{O}(X)\times\mathcal{O}(Y)\subseteq\mathcal{O}(X)\times\mathcal{O}(Y)\setminus\{(X,Y)\},

a contradiction. Thus,X×Y∈𝒰X\times Y\in\mathcal{U}andX×YX\times Yis weaklyS​ISI-compact.∎

Finally, we will consider theS​ISI-compactness of powerspaces.

## Proposition 6.11.

LetXXbe a topological space. Then the following statements hold.

(1)ifXXis compact thenPS​(X)P_{S}(X)is SI-compact;

(2)ifPS​(X)P_{S}(X)is compact, thenXXis compact.

## Proof.

(1) Let𝒰⊆𝒪​(PS​(X))\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(P_{S}(X))be a Scott irreducible open cover of𝒬​(X)\mathcal{Q}(X). SinceXXis compact, or equivalentlyX∈𝒬​(X)X\in\mathcal{Q}(X),X∈⋃i∈I□​Ui∈𝒰X\in\bigcup\limits_{i\in I}\Box U_{i}\in\mathcal{U}. So𝒬​(X)=⋃i∈I□​Ui∈𝒰\mathcal{Q}(X)=\bigcup\limits_{i\in I}\Box U_{i}\in\mathcal{U}and hencePS​(X)P_{S}(X)is SI-compact.

(2)Let𝒰⊆𝒪​(X)\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(X)be an open cover ofXX. The collection of all sets having the form□​(⋃ℱ)\Box(\bigcup\mathcal{F})for a finite subsetℱ⊆𝒰\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{U}is denoted by𝒱\mathcal{V}. For everyK∈𝒬​(X)K\in\mathcal{Q}(X),K⊆⋃𝒰K\subseteq\bigcup\mathcal{U}and henceK⊆⋃ℱ0K\subseteq\bigcup\mathcal{F}_{0}for some finite subsetℱ0⊆𝒰\mathcal{F}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{U}by the compactness ofKK. This means that𝒱\mathcal{V}covers𝒬​(X)\mathcal{Q}(X). AsPS​(X)P_{S}(X)is compact, there are finite subsetsℱ1,ℱ2,⋯,ℱn⊆𝒰\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2},\cdot\cdot\cdot,\mathcal{F}_{n}\subseteq\mathcal{U}such that𝒬​(X)⊆(□​(⋃ℱ1))∪(□​(⋃ℱ2))∪⋯∪(□​(⋃ℱn)).\mathcal{Q}(X)\subseteq(\Box(\bigcup\mathcal{F}_{1}))\cup(\Box(\bigcup\mathcal{F}_{2}))\cup\cdot\cdot\cdot\cup(\Box(\bigcup\mathcal{F}_{n})).

So we haveX=⋃𝒬(X)⊆(⋃ℱ1)∪(⋃ℱ2))∪⋅⋅∪(⋃ℱn)).X=\bigcup\mathcal{Q}(X)\subseteq(\bigcup\mathcal{F}_{1})\cup(\bigcup\mathcal{F}_{2}))\cup\cdot\cdot\cup(\bigcup\mathcal{F}_{n})).

Therefore,XXis compact.∎

## Corollary 6.12.

LetXXbe a topological space. Then the following statements are equivalent.

(1)XXis compact;

(2)PS​(X)P_{S}(X)is compact;

(3)PS​(X)P_{S}(X)is SI-compact.

Unfortunately, the SI-compactness ofPS​(X)P_{S}(X)does not imply the SI-compactness ofXX(See example 6.13).

## Example 6.13.

LetXXbe a compact and non SI-compact space. By Proposition 6.12,PS​(X)P_{S}(X)is SI-compact butXXis not.

## Proposition 6.14.

LetXXbe a topological space. Then the following statements are equivalent.

(a)XXis a SI-compact space;

(b)PH​(X)P_{H}(X)is SI-compact.

## Proof.

(a)⟹(b){\rm(a)\Longrightarrow(b)}Note thatξ:X⟶PH​(X)\xi:X\longrightarrow P_{H}(X)is continuous, whereξ​(x)=c​l​({x})\xi(x)=cl(\{x\}), for allx∈X\ x\in X. Thusξ−1:𝒪​(PH​(X))⟶𝒪​(X)\xi^{-1}:\mathcal{O}(P_{H}(X))\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}(X)is continuous with respect to the Scott topology. Supposeℱ⊆𝒪​(PH​(X))\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(P_{H}(X))is a Scott irreducible open cover ofΓ0​(X)\Gamma_{0}(X). Thenξ−1​(ℱ)\xi^{-1}(\mathcal{F})is also irreducible. Clearly,ξ−1​(ℱ)\xi^{-1}(\mathcal{F})coversXXsince{c​l​({x})∣x∈X}⊆⋃ℱ\{cl(\{x\})\mid x\in X\}\subseteq\bigcup\mathcal{F}. It follows from the SI-compactness ofXXthatX∈ξ−1​(ℱ)X\in\xi^{-1}(\mathcal{F}). Choose aF∈ℱF\in\mathcal{F}withX=ξ−1​(F)X=\xi^{-1}(F). LetF=⋃i∈I(⋂j∈Ji⋄Uj)F=\bigcup\limits_{i\in I}(\bigcap\limits_{j\in J_{i}}\diamond U_{j}), whereJiJ_{i}is finite for eachi∈Ii\in Iand{Uj∣j∈Ji,i∈I}⊆𝒪​(X)\{U_{j}\mid j\in J_{i},i\in I\}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(X). IfA∈Γ0​(X)A\in\Gamma_{0}(X)anda∈Aa\in A, thena∈ξ−1​(ℱ)a\in\xi^{-1}(\mathcal{F})and hencec​l​({a})∈Fcl(\{a\})\in F. Clearly,FFis upper inΓ0​(X)\Gamma_{0}(X). Then we haveA∈FA\in F. This impliesΓ0​(X)=F\Gamma_{0}(X)=F. Therefore,PH​(X)P_{H}(X)is SI-compact.

(b)⟹(a){\rm(b)\Longrightarrow(a)}Define a mapη:Σ​(𝒪​(X))⟶Σ​(𝒪​(PH​(X)))\eta:\Sigma(\mathcal{O}(X))\longrightarrow\Sigma(\mathcal{O}(P_{H}(X)))byη​(U)=⋄U\eta(U)=\diamond U. Obviously,η\etais continuous sinceη​(U)\eta(U)preserves arbitrary unions. Letℰ⊆𝒪​(X)\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(X)be a Scott irreducible open cover ofXX. Thenη​(ℰ)\eta(\mathcal{E})is also irreducible inΣ​(𝒪​(PH​(X)))\Sigma(\mathcal{O}(P_{H}(X))). IfB∈Γ0​(X)B\in\Gamma_{0}(X)andb∈Bb\in B, thenb∈Eb\in Efor someE∈ℰE\in\mathcal{E}. So we haveB∈⋄EB\in\diamond Eand henceΓ0​(X)=⋃η​(ℰ)\Gamma_{0}(X)=\bigcup\eta(\mathcal{E}). AsPH​(X)P_{H}(X)is SI-compact,Γ0​(X)∈η​(ℰ)\Gamma_{0}(X)\in\eta(\mathcal{E}). Take aE0∈ℰE_{0}\in\mathcal{E}such thatΓ0​(X)=⋄E0\Gamma_{0}(X)=\diamond E_{0}. Especially,{c​l​({x})∣x∈X}⊆⋄E0\{cl(\{x\})\mid x\in X\}\subseteq\diamond E_{0}. It is easy to deduce thatX=E0X=E_{0}. ThusXXis SI-compact.∎

## 7Conclusions

In this paper, we mainly discussed theS​ISI-compactness andυ\upsilon-compactness of topological spaces. Specifically, we gave a new method for obtaining a Scott non sober frame by using the nonS​ISI-compact space and we further got a countableT1T_{1}space whose open set lattice is Scott non sober. However, we still did not know the answers of the following questions.

## Problem 7.1.

Is everyσ𝒞\sigma_{\mathcal{C}}-compact spaceS​ISI-compact?

## Problem 7.2.

Is(P^,ω​(P^))(\widehat{P},\omega(\widehat{P}))sober for the dcpoP^\widehat{P}presented in Example 4.8?

## Problem 7.3.

LetXXbe a countable Hausdorff space. IsΣ​𝒪​(X)\Sigma\mathcal{O}(X)sober? In particular, for the rational numbers subspaceℚ\mathbb{Q}of real lineℝ\mathbb{R}, isΣ​𝒪​(ℚ)\Sigma\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{Q})sober?

## Problem 7.4.

LetXXandYYbe a pair of compact spaces such thatΣ​𝒪​(X)\Sigma\mathcal{O}(X)andΣ​𝒪​(Y)\Sigma\mathcal{O}(Y)are sober. IsX×YX\times YalwaysS​ISI-compact?
